### Preface
## To the Art-Loving Reader.

<image-desc>The entire page is framed by a decorative border made of interlocking oval links. Below the page number, a thin horizontal line and a wide decorative band composed of repeated typographic fleurons separate the header area from the main titles. The main text begins with a large, elaborate woodcut initial 'E' featuring scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

Art-loving reader, Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), an influential Cistercian abbot and mystic known for his writings on spiritual discipline.</note> writes well and rightly in his letters <note>original: "Episteln"</note>: that a noble lineage, a fit body, beautiful form, quick intellect, the application of skill, and the highly-praised integrity of good morals <note>original: "Sitten"</note> are indeed worth something. However, Chrysostom <note>Saint John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an early Church Father renowned for his eloquent preaching.</note> adds in his sermons <note>original: "Homiliis"</note> that the goodness of one's relatives helps no one else; rather, we must be good for ourselves, and if we wish to accomplish anything good and fruitful, we must put our own hands to the work.

<summary>This preface introduces the book by citing religious authorities to argue that personal effort and individual virtue are more valuable than inherited status or natural talent. It sets a moral tone, encouraging the reader to take an active role in their own improvement and craft.</summary>

<keywords>preface, Bernard of Clairvaux, John Chrysostom, virtue, personal effort, morality, art-loving reader</keywords>

# Preface.

<meta>The text is enclosed within a rectangular ornamental border composed of small linked oval motifs.</meta>

->Preface.<-

Since then (as Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a highly influential theologian and philosopher.</note> reports) to /
wander astray anywhere is very dangerous /
to seek something is very laborious / 
but when one has found it /
such a thing is very fruitful and good:
Therefore, at the request of the
publisher, I have undertaken to take 
this **Chemical Treatise**,
which THOMAS NORTON,
an Englishman / many years ago / in 
his mother tongue / wrote in the form
of rhymes / and titled *Believe Me* <note>original Latin: "CREDE MIHI"</note> or 
*The Ordinal* <note>original Latin: "ORDINALE"</note>, which 
afterwards was translated from the 
English manuscript into Latin and 
printed by the late Doctor Michael 
Maier <note>Michael Maier (1568–1622), a famous German physician and alchemist who served as counselor to Emperor Rudolf II.</note>, Count Palatine <note>original Latin: "Com: Pal:", short for Comes Palatinus, a title of nobility.</note> / I have undertaken / 
I say / to bring this work from the 
said Latin into common German 
rhymes / especially because the said 
treatise is highly esteemed by many 
as being very good and useful.

<meta>catchword: Since-</meta>

->Preface.<-

However, since the aforementioned Norton <note>Thomas Norton (c. 1433–1513), an English alchemist and poet whose "Ordinal" is a foundational text of the Western alchemical tradition.</note> made use of a particular style in the manner of English verse—which the previously mentioned Mr. Maier <note>Michael Maier (1568–1622), physician to Rudolf II, who translated Norton’s work into Latin in his 1618 "Tripus Aureus."</note> also followed strictly in his own translation <note>original: "Translation"</note>—I have likewise felt compelled to adhere to Mr. Maier’s version <note>original: "Version"</note> and remain strictly <note>original Latin: "strictè"</note> faithful to the text. Otherwise, I would have provided the kind reader with more elegant rhymes <note>original: "Rhythmos"</note> and verses according to my own preference.

Regarding the illustrations <note>original: "Figuren"; these refer to the symbolic alchemical emblems often included in such manuscripts to encode chemical processes.</note> placed before each chapter: although at the beginning of the text they may appear as if they do not fit the subject, or as if they concern something other than what is discussed in that chapter, the kind reader will eventually—once they have read the entire chapter with careful consideration—unfailingly

# Preface.

<image-desc>The text is enclosed within a decorative rectangular border composed of repeating oval and circular chain-like patterns.</image-desc>

...unfailingly <note>The syllable "barlich" completes a word from the previous page, likely "unfehlbarlich" (unfailingly) or "offenbarlich" (evidently).</note> find that the text and figures <note>original: "Schrifft vnd Figuren"; referring to the technical descriptions and the alchemical emblems.</note> correspond well and fit together properly.

I am, therefore, in certain hope and good confidence that the art-seeking reader <note>A term for students of the "Great Art" (Alchemy) seeking hidden knowledge.</note> will derive sufficient delight and great benefit from this work. I trust that—since it is very dangerous to stray into error, as mentioned above—the reader will finally find that which he has laboriously sought herein: a fruitful, convenient, and good thing, which he may use profitably. I felt it my duty to indicate this to the favorable reader. Herewith, I commend us all to the merciful protection of God. Given at Frankfurt am Main, the 6th of February, <term>in the year 1625.</term>

### EPIGRAM
### BY MICHAEL MAIER,

## On NORTON’S Chemical Treatise.

Just as the Nile with its flow
Must moisten the earth and land,
And pours out everywhere, without rushing, <note>original: "ohn sauß"; meaning steadily or without wild noise.</note>
Its gathered waters,
And carries with it a fertile silt <note>A reference to the nutrient-rich mud deposited by the Nile floods, essential for Egyptian agriculture.</note>
With rich, natural, and fine fruit of the earth,
So that in the future, above all,
At harvest time, one may have good hope:
In the same way, Norton comes forward here
With a broad millstone, <note>This likely refers to the "grinding" or processing of alchemical materials, or metaphorically to the weight and foundational nature of his work.</note>
Because he has first of all
Properly opened up the Noble Work <note>The "Noble Work" refers to the *Magnum Opus* or the "Great Work" of alchemy—the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of Nature:
With an immeasurable breadth
He spreads himself far and wide,
So that he may freely make fertile
The chemical <term>fields</term> <note>A metaphor for the mind of the alchemist or the practice of the art itself.</note> and bring joy to the artists. <note>In 17th-century texts, "artists" (Künstlr) specifically refers to practitioners of alchemy, the "Hermetic Art."</note>

<summary>This page features a commendatory poem by the famous physician and alchemist Michael Maier, using the metaphor of the flooding Nile to describe how Thomas Norton’s writings enrich and "fertilize" the field of alchemy for other practitioners.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Maier, Thomas Norton, alchemy, Nile metaphor, Great Work, chemical fields, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

> Be able to have your wish <note>In an alchemical context, "the wish" (der Wunsch) refers to the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the attainment of perfect knowledge.</note> forevermore.

Is not shown in a single book for that reason:

# THOMAS NORTON’S
### Chemical Treatise /
### Called *Crede Mihi* or *The Ordinal*.
<note>The Latin title "Crede mihi" translates to "Believe me." The "Ordinal" refers to Norton’s most famous work, the *Ordinal of Alchemy*, composed around 1477.</note>

->Author’s Preface.<-

This book demonstrates science <note>original: "Wissenschafft"; here meaning the specialized, secret knowledge of alchemy.</note> very well
To the clergy <note>original: "Clericis"; referring to the learned or priestly class.</note> quite rightly:
This book increases at all times
The ignorance of the laity: <note>original: "Layen"; the unlearned or common people. Norton suggests the text is written in a way that remains a mystery to those without proper spiritual or intellectual preparation.</note>
This is a book of pure honors,
Through its abundance it helps most finely:
This book drives far away
The incapacity <note>original: "Unvermögn"; can mean poverty, lack of resources, or inability.</note> of poor people.
It is a book of confidence
And of truth (of which there is no lack.)
A counsel to kings even more,
And a lesson to the prelates: <note>High-ranking church officials.</note>
It is a book which, for the time,
Is useful to godly men,
Who desire to live without sin,

<summary>This page serves as the title and opening preface for Thomas Norton's alchemical masterpiece, describing the "Ordinal" as a source of truth and wealth. It frames alchemy as a sacred science intended for the clergy and the godly, while remaining hidden from the unlearned.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Norton, Ordinal of Alchemy, Crede Mihi, alchemy, clergy, laity, sacred science, poverty, kings and prelates</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuity check: This page continues the versified preface to Thomas Norton's "Ordinal of Alchemy" (Crede Mihi). It elaborates on the spiritual and moral requirements of the alchemist, warning against "false" seekers driven by greed and emphasizing that only a few "chosen" individuals will succeed through faith, labor, and gratitude.</meta>

It is a secret book, and alongside that,
A book so full of God’s gift,
A footpath of good hope, especially
For chosen men, so steadfast,
Full of the power of strong faith:
Who at all hours believe the words
That proceed out of my mouth.
The true and the false seekers here
Both commonly seek after Alchemy: <note>original: "Alchymi"</note>
The false ones are many, beyond number,
But they are all rejected
And are (alas), as is plainly seen,
Infected with many desires. <note>This refers to the "lusts" of the flesh or greed, which alchemical tradition taught would prevent a seeker from ever achieving the Stone.</note>
So that scarcely three are chosen
Among a thousand times a thousand, in general. <note>A common alchemical trope emphasizing that while many try, almost none succeed because they lack the proper spiritual purity.</note>
To this science <note>original: "Wissenschafft"; here meaning the specialized, secret knowledge of the "sacred art" of alchemy.</note> many are
Called, yet with measure and purpose.
Noble, ignoble, poor and rich,
Learned and unskilled alike.
But those who, upon this path, <note>original: "Plan"; literally a level ground or field, here metaphorically the field of study.</note>
Do not invest time and labor,
They will not complete the Work, <note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) or the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>
Because they are ungrateful in all things.
This book teaches sufficiently, by favor,
The sons of this noble art: <note>original: "Söhne dieser edlen Kunst"; a traditional term for initiated alchemists who were seen as a spiritual family.</note>
To the grateful, skillfully and beautifully

God has given it to be understood.
Believe these four little verses, <note>original: "Versiculis"; a Latin diminutive for short verses of poetry.</note>
Which are prophetic and surely true.
The fountain of Divine Majesty
Gives everything for free, out of pure grace. <note>This refers to the concept of the *donum Dei* (the gift of God), the belief that the Philosopher's Stone cannot be achieved by labor alone, but must be granted by God to the virtuous.</note>

This Work would have often graced, in time,
The Kings in England. <note>Thomas Norton was an English alchemist; this refers to the historical interest (and often failures) of English monarchs like Edward III or Henry VI in using alchemy to fund the state.</note>

In God the Lord.

Receive the honor by and by,
Will change <note>original: "mutirn"; from the Latin *mutare*, to change or transform.</note> the old customs,

With many virtues alongside,
He will give a very good example <note>original: "Exempel"</note>
To the kings, which shall remain forever,

### The Second Preface.

To the praise of the one God,
Who is Threefold in Persons above, <note>A reference to the Holy Trinity, emphasizing that alchemy was viewed as a divine, Christian science.</note>
This book is truly made
So that the learned host,
As well as the simple unlearned, <note>original: "Vngelehrte schlecht"; "schlecht" here means "simple" or "plain" rather than "bad."</note>
Might see it rightly after my death:
And so that all inexperienced people
Who constantly presume
To seek great wealth and money
Through alchemy here in this world,
May find good counsel
Before they rightly begin the Work.

From which they then make false silver

## The First Chapter.

<image-desc>An alchemical engraving labeled "2." in the upper left corner. In the center, a bearded man (likely a philosopher or Hermes Trismegistus) stands inside a large celestial sphere or globe marked with zodiacal or astronomical bands. To his left stands a small, naked child or homunculus. To his right stands a man in Roman-style military armor. In the background, a landscape features a church or tower on the left and rolling hills on the right.</image-desc>

The <term>Mastery</term>, great and wondrous, <note>original: "Meisterschafft." Refers to the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus), the successful completion of the alchemical process.</note>
And the Arch-Mastery so clear,
Is the <term>Tincture</term> (mark me well) <note>original: "Tinctur." In alchemy, this is the refined essence or "medicine" capable of transmuting base metals into gold or silver.</note>
Of the most holy <term>Alchemy</term>, <note>original: "Alchymi."</note>
A rare science is she
Of secret <term>Philosophy</term>, <note>original: "Philosophi."</note>
A special grace and gift
Sent down from the eternal God in heaven,
Which by no man, at any hour,
Was ever through manual labor found. <note>This reflects a core alchemical belief: the "Stone" cannot be created through mere chemical recipes (manual labor) alone, but requires divine revelation or "illumination."</note>

<summary>This page introduces the first chapter, defining alchemy as a "holy science" and a divine gift from God that cannot be achieved through physical work alone without spiritual grace.</summary>
<keywords>The First Chapter, Mastery, Tincture, Alchemy, Philosophy, Divine Gift, Manual Labor</keywords>

Only through discovery and teaching
->Does it come to them from other people;<-
This has never into any man's
->Hand come to be sold,<-

->By him who often seeks it day and night:<-
But through <term>God’s grace</term> <note>original: "Gottes Gnad." A central alchemical tenet is that the "Donum Dei" (Gift of God) is required for success.</note> alone
->Is it finely given to the worthy,<-

->Takes away all transitory honor,<-

->Quickly with the humblest moderation.<-
Some consider it unworthy
->To call such an <term>Art</term> "holy,"<- <note>original: "Kunst." Alchemists often called their work the "Sacred Art."</note>
Just as in this way and manner
->They diligently argue,<-
Saying that the accursed
->Have used such <term>Science</term> at all times.<- <note>original: "Wissenschafft." At this time, "science" referred to any organized body of knowledge or a specific branch of learning.</note>

<meta>This page continues Thomas Norton’s "Ordinal of Alchemy" (translated into German). It addresses the character of those who are barred from the Art—specifically those motivated by greed and worldly wealth—and reaffirms that the "holy science" is only granted by God to the pious and deserving.</meta>

<image-desc>The text is enclosed within a decorative rectangular border composed of repeating oval and circular chain-like ornaments.</image-desc>

[Those] obtain [it], to whom exactly

<meta>This page continues the German verse translation of Thomas Norton’s "Ordinal of Alchemy." While the previous page ended with the catchword "Wann" (When), this page begins with the conclusion of the thought regarding the rejection of worldly ambition, emphasizing that the "Art" is not a birthright but a reward for virtue. It outlines the strict "rule of one"—the tradition that an alchemist may only pass their knowledge to a single successor.</meta>

<image-desc>The text is enclosed within a decorative rectangular border composed of repeating oval and circular chain-link motifs.</image-desc>

One is not to be chosen (understand me)
->For this great <term>dignity</term>;<- <note>original: "Dignität." In this context, it refers to the high spiritual and social status of a true Master of Alchemy.</note>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the selection of an alchemical successor. While the previous page discussed the master's duty to find a worthy student in their old age, this page explains the catastrophic social and spiritual consequences of teaching the "Art" to a wicked or greedy individual. It emphasizes that alchemy is a divine gift reserved only for the virtuous to prevent the subversion of the natural and social order.</meta>

If a wicked man were to know such an **Art**, <note>original: "Kunst." In this context, it refers to the Great Work of alchemy, the ability to transmute base metals into gold.</note>
Great misfortune would surely follow;
Also, much danger and suffering would strike
God's community and all of **Christendom**.
For he would be puffed up with **greed**, <note>original: "Geitz," meaning avarice. Greed was considered the primary disqualifier for an alchemist.</note>
And would cast down the rightful lords
From their **inheritances** in such a way, <note>original: "Erbschafften." The author fears that alchemical gold would undermine the hereditary wealth and authority of the ruling nobility.</note>
That henceforth those who now
Have command over others [would be displaced]:
For which things, punishment and danger
Would be threatened forevermore
Against the one who—however worthy—
Had taught the Art to such a person.
Therefore, one should certainly be on guard
Because of the **suspicion** <note>original: "Suspicion," from the Latin *suspicio*. This refers to the need for the master to be wary of any hidden flaws in a potential student's character.</note>
Of such splendor, **pride**, and **arrogance** <note>original: "Hoffart" and "Ubermuht." These terms describe the vanity that leads one to believe they are above God's laws.</note>
In the one to whom such an art is handed over;
For this reason, absolutely no single man
Can receive such a heartfelt gift,
Except for he alone who
Shines so brightly with **virtues**. <note>original: "Tugenden." This reinforces the "Donum Dei" (Gift of God) concept: alchemy is a reward for moral excellence, not just intellectual study.</note>
Now, if they themselves should think,
In opposition to the practitioners of the Art here,
To deny that such a **science** <note>original: "Wissenschafft." At this time, alchemy was viewed as a sacred and rigorous branch of natural philosophy.</note>
Is holy, as was previously argued,
Or that it does not, from its results,
Appear to be so truly blessed—

<column-break/>

->So<- <note>This is the catchword, a printing convention where the first word of the following page is printed at the bottom right to ensure the pages are bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page warns that sharing alchemical secrets with a greedy or arrogant person would lead to the collapse of social hierarchies and the punishment of the teacher. It asserts that the "Art" is a holy science reserved exclusively for those of exceptional virtue.</summary>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the spiritual nature of alchemy. While the previous page warned that the "Art" must be kept from the wicked and greedy to prevent social upheaval, this page explains why the Art is inherently "holy": it is a divine gift that transcends human intellect and social status, requiring God's direct grace to achieve the transmutation of base metals into gold.</meta>

So it is, according to its order,
->That it is holy and truly high:<- <note>The author refers to the "Order" of alchemy as a sacred hierarchy or system established by God.</note>
Because no one can find the same,
->Regardless of the rank they hold,<- <note>original: "Stand." This reinforces the idea that neither nobility nor wealth can buy alchemical success; it is a spiritual meritocracy.</note>
Unless it be through **Divine Grace**;
->Therefore, it bears a holy name.<-
Thus it is also a fine work
->And a **Divine labor** (mark me well):<-
From base **copper**, such as it is,
->To make fine **gold** and **silver**.<-
No one will find such a rich transformation
->Through their own thoughts alone,<- <note>The text warns that the "Great Work" cannot be reasoned out by human logic; it requires a "revelation" or divine spark.</note>
Out of many various things
->Which God has created so freely.<-
For what God has brought together,
->Man should not **disjoin**:<- <note>original: "disiungirn," from the Latin *disiungere*. This is a play on the biblical phrase "What God has joined, let no man put asunder," applied here to the chemical separation (*separatio*) of elements.</note>
Unless it be that, perfectly indeed,
->His gracious **consent** is also there,<- <note>original: "Consens," from the Latin *consensus*.</note>
With the wealth of such **science**, <note>original: "Wissenschafft." In this period, "science" referred to a systematic body of knowledge or the state of "knowing" granted by God.</note>
->Which God the Lord, through His power,<-
Grants to those through grace
->Whom He has loved at all times.<-
Therefore, the ancient **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophi." This refers to the "Adepts" or legendary alchemists of the past who were seen as both scientists and saints.</note>
->Have not unjustly named here<-
This Art, always and at every time,
->An **Alchemy** that is holy.<-

<summary>This page defines alchemy as a "Holy Art" that cannot be mastered through intellect or status alone, but only through God's grace. It describes the transmutation of copper into gold as a divine labor that requires celestial consent to "disjoin" what God has created.</summary>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->For if it were granted to them<-
To possess such an **Art**, without doubt, <note>original: "Kunst," referring to the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy. "Without doubt" translates the Latin *ohn dubio* in the text.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->They would never show it to anyone in this way.<- <note>This is a common logical argument in alchemical literature: if an alchemist truly knew how to make gold, he would be the wealthiest man on earth and would not need to beg for funds or sell his secrets.</note>
And when they **promise** someone <note>original: "promittirn." From the Latin *promittere*.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->The augmentation and **multiplication**,<- <note>original: "Multiplicirn." In alchemy, Multiplication is the stage where the power and quantity of the Philosopher's Stone is increased.</note>
They are planning for them (believe me)
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->An act of villainy and roguery,<-

<summary>This page warns the reader that greed makes men vulnerable to "Multipliers"—fraudulent alchemists who use false oaths to steal from their patrons. It argues that a true possessor of the alchemical Art would never need to solicit money from others.</summary>

<keywords>deceit, greed, Multiplier, false oaths, Multiplication, fraud, alchemical Art, poverty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of "multipliers"—fraudulent alchemists who claim to increase the quantity of precious metals. To rob others of their goods/
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And to deaden good intentions/
What more should I always say/
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And complain about such fellows?
Such **falsifiers** (understand me well) <note>original: "Fälscher." This refers to both counterfeiters of coins and "sophist" alchemists who use tricks to make base metals appear like gold.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So that there would not be so many of them:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They gather together false lies/

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Multiplied** (as I report) <note>original: "Multiplicirt." In alchemy, "Multiplication" is the specific process of increasing the quantity or the transformative power of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Everyone should be quite certain/
That **Nature**, with her beauty/
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Shows and **displays** more Art, <note>original: "ostentir," from the Latin *ostentare*, meaning to show off or exhibit.</note>
She multiplies nothing here at all
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;(As many **authors** tell you) <note>original: "Authores." The established writers and "Masters" of the alchemical tradition.</note>
Unless it be only one truly right way/
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Out of these two simple ways:
Either from **putrefaction** <note>original: "Faulbehendt," glossed in the next line as "Faulmachung" (the making-rotten). In alchemy, *putrefactio* is the essential first stage where matter must die and decay (turning black) before it can be reborn and perfected.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which one calls "the making-rotten"/

<summary>The author argues that alchemical "multiplication" is a fraud because it contradicts the laws of Nature. He explains that increase only occurs through two natural paths—putrefaction or biological procreation—and asserts that metals are incapable of the latter.</summary>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 39. <page-num>40</page-num>

But in our **Stone** you shall
> See much that is similar to it, with beauty;
**Putrefaction**, <note>original: "Fäulung." In alchemy, this refers to the *putrefactio* or *nigredo* (blackening) stage, where the matter must "die" and decompose before it can be purified and reborn.</note> as I now speak,

> And born within the **earth**; <note>The author refers to the common alchemical belief that metals "grow" in the womb of the earth over centuries, nurtured by subterranean heat.</note>

From all of which, at any time,

But above the earth there,
> They are not at all in their [proper] place,
For the places of **Nature**
> Are merely striving against one another;
Just as the fish, great and small,
> Are soon dead outside of the water,

> With the birds and other animals:

<summary>The author argues that metals belong in the earth and decay into rust or verdigris when exposed to the surface air. He uses the analogy of fish dying out of water to explain that the alchemical process must respect the natural environments ("places") established by Nature.</summary>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the natural "place" of metals. The **Apothecaries** and **Physicians**
->Have absolutely no desire here<-
To seek the little water-streams
->On dry hills and little mountains.<- <note>The author uses this analogy to suggest that just as one does not look for water in a dry place, one should not expect things to grow outside of their natural environment.</note>

->By His wisdom and great grace,<-

->In vessels that are properly prepared?<- <note>The "multipliers" argue that if Nature can grow metals in the earth, a chemist should be able to replicate that growth in a laboratory flask (vessel) if it is "rightly prepared."</note>

<summary>The author describes how some argue that because metal veins in the earth vary in richness, metals must naturally "grow" and multiply, leading to the alchemical claim that this growth can be replicated in laboratory vessels.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate from page 41. The author responds to the "multipliers"—those who believe they can grow metals in laboratory flasks—by arguing that the specific astrological and geological conditions of the earth cannot be replicated by human artifice.</meta>

They preserve [them] so strictly <note>Likely referring to the vessels or conditions mentioned on the previous page.</note>
->From water, the air, and the fire?<-
To this we now answer openly,
->That this method is in vain,<-
Because the comparison at this time
->Is not at all perfect:<- <note>The author argues that comparing a glass laboratory vessel to the vast, living earth is a false equivalence.</note>

<summary>The author refutes the idea that metals can be grown in labs, arguing that metal formation requires specific "select grounds" and constant celestial influence from the heavens that cannot be replicated in a vessel.</summary>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of "multiplication," the alchemical belief that metals can be grown or increased in quantity through laboratory processes. Having previously argued that metals require specific celestial influences, the author now turns to a geological analogy involving ice to illustrate why metals cannot be artificially expanded.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

So that all may understand it together,
->Those who are wise and go about with cleverness,<-

->Or how it truly behaves with the ice,<-
Before it becomes quite wonderfully hard;
Some is indeed more abundant
->In its [natural] places, as is customary,<- <note original: "nach Gebrauch">Literally "according to use" or "custom," here referring to the natural habit or behavior of the substance.</note>

->Just as the waters are to be reckoned,<-
In the pits and in the brooks.
It was a small quantity
->Before it began to become hard;<-
But the waters in the great lakes
->Simply have larger **veins**,<- <term>veins: (Adern) in historical mining and alchemy, these were the subterranean channels or cracks in the rock where "mineralizing vapors" or waters were thought to flow and eventually solidify into ore.</term>
Because of the abundance in them there
Of the **congealed matter**, <note original: "Congelirtn Materia">In alchemy, "congealment" is the transition of a substance from a fluid or gaseous state into a solid; the author compares the freezing of ice to the solidification of minerals.</note>
Therefore it would not be consistent
->If one wished to say, as a rule,<-
That one could therefore
->**Multiply** the ice quite quickly.<- <note original: "Multipliciren">A central alchemical concept where a practitioner attempts to increase the mass or power of a substance, such as turning a small amount of gold into a larger amount.</note>
Although the veins of the **metals**
->Are sometimes more or less in size,<-
It does not follow that they should produce more
->Than they were previously in their own nature.<-
Thus you must now believe rightly,
->Without any doubt and quite simply:<-

<summary>The author employs a natural analogy, comparing the formation of metals to the freezing of water into ice. He argues that the size of a mineral deposit is determined by the initial amount of "congealed matter" present, just as the volume of ice depends on the initial water, thereby refuting the possibility of alchemical multiplication.</summary>

That all things that enter in

->Take heed, as you surely notice:<- <term>composition: (Composition) the specific combination of elements or qualities that make up a substance.</term>

<summary>The author argues that while plants can multiply through seeds, metals have a fixed composition; even when dissolved in acid, an ounce of silver remains an ounce of silver and cannot be "multiplied."</summary>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of "multipliers"—alchemists who believe that metals can be grown like crops. Unless there be a sensing kind
Or a vegetative law,
Since the **metals**, great and small,
Are purely **elemental**;
They indeed have no **seed**,
Nor any consciousness of the body:
Therefore we conclude that the
**Multipliers** <note>Multipliers (Vielfaltigmacher) were alchemists who claimed they could increase the physical quantity of a metal as if it were a living thing that could reproduce.</note> should cease here,
For once a **metal** is born,
It grows no more, in any case:
Nevertheless (believe me),
We do find a **changed metal**,
Into another kind of metal,
Because of the true proximity of the **matter**
Which was proven to be within them,
As is known between **copper** and **iron**; <note>This likely refers to the "transmutation" of iron into copper observed in mine waters (cementation), where iron objects placed in copper-rich water appear to turn into copper. But to make true **gold** and **silver**,
One can do nothing at all in such matters,
Unless it be (as is beautifully known)
By the **Philosophers’ Medicine**. <term>Philosophers' Medicine: A synonym for the Philosopher's Stone or Tincture; it was viewed as a "medicine" that healed "sick" or "imperfect" base metals, turning them into "healthy" gold.</term>
Therefore, those works
Which the **multipliers** (take note)
Employ, all the learned reject,
And they shun them to the utmost,
But the **holy Alchemy**
Is rightly to be highly honored here.

<summary>The author argues that because metals lack biological seeds, they cannot "grow" or multiply like plants; however, they can be changed into one another through their shared material nature or perfected into gold using the Philosophers' Medicine.</summary>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on alchemical wisdom and the "Chemical King" introduced on page 48. The text moves from the mythological figure of Hermes to the philosophical requirements of a true practitioner, emphasizing the distinction between true "science" and mere "imagination." The catchword "Ist" (Is) suggests the following page begins with that word.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>

Is upright and natural,
Just as the four <note>Likely referring to the four Aristotelian elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water, which were the foundation of natural philosophy.</note> in Nature
Reveal themselves purely with their **sciences**; <term>Sciences: original 'Wissenschafftn'. In this period, this refers to organized bodies of knowledge or the "Art" of alchemy, rather than modern empirical science.</term>
And there, he says without restraint,
One will consider that man blessed

That which belongs
To such a good science:
For many, by their own opinion,
Are deceived beyond measure or end,
Concerning those things which they
Have never truly recognized.
(It is an old proverb) that there may not be
In a large heap—freely
Full of **imagination**— <note>In alchemical literature, "imagination" often refers to false fantasies or "puffers" (amateur alchemists) who imagine results without following the true laws of Nature.</note>

With proper **proof** <term>Proof: original 'Probation'. The technical process of testing a substance's purity or verifying an alchemical claim through experiment.</term>
And sufficient **discretion**, <term>Discretion: original 'Discretion'. The intellectual and spiritual ability to discern between true and false paths in the Great Work.</term>
The **wise ones** <term>Wise ones: original 'Die Weissen'. A standard term for the "Sages" or "Philosophers" of alchemy who have attained the Stone.</term> also daily
Learn much more, according to their custom.
Truly, through science
Mankind knows itself
And all things; a **beast** <note>original: "Bestia". Latin for beast or animal. The text likely continues by contrasting human reason and science with the irrationality of animals.</note>

<summary>This page defines the "true science" of alchemy as a disciplined pursuit requiring discernment and proof, contrasting it with the "imagination" of those who are easily deceived. It emphasizes that true knowledge allows a person to understand both the natural world and themselves.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Philosophy, Four Elements, Science (Wissenschafft), Proof (Probation), Discretion, Sages (Weissen), Self-knowledge, Reason vs. Instinct</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 49 regarding the value of true knowledge over mere imagination. The text transitions from the general necessity of "science" to the specific educational tradition of the Seven Liberal Arts, emphasizing that true learning requires a noble spirit. The catchword "Ist" from the previous page correctly connects to the opening line here.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>

Is man indeed without **science**? <term>Science: original 'Wissenschafft'. In this historical context, it refers to a systematic body of knowledge or a specific "Art" (like alchemy or logic) rather than modern laboratory science.</term>
But hardly with a desire
Is one guided toward that **science**
Of which he himself, before everyone,
Knows nothing, nor can understand;
The lack of **skill** <term>Skill: original 'Geschickligkeit'. This implies a natural aptitude or a readiness to receive instruction.</term>
Makes one bold and wild at all times,
But sufficient **science**
Makes one affable and virtuous.
The noble are now accustomed to despise
Those who strive to know such things.
The Kings of olden times
Have decreed, far and wide
(As many **authors** <note>original: 'Auctores'</note> testify at hand)
That no one at all was permitted to learn
The **seven liberal sciences**, <note>The 'Seven Liberal Arts' (Trivium and Quadrivium) which formed the basis of medieval and Renaissance education: Grammar, Rhetoric, Logic, Arithmetic, Geometry, Music, and Astronomy.</note>
Unless he be of very fine mind,
Of a noble **genius**, <note>original: 'Ingenii'. Referring to one's natural character, intellect, or innate spirit.</note>
So liberal and praiseworthy here.
And whoever is devoted to the same
Can live in the freest manner;
In his free **studies** <note>original: 'Studiis'</note>
He could certainly remain.
Therefore the ancients have
Held them to be seven liberal sciences,
For whoever wished to learn them well

->Perfect<- <note>Catchword for the following page: 'Perfect'.</note>

Then he is worth much at such a time.
**Science** is therefore,
And many other gifts besides,
Corrupted and entirely lost.

## The Second Chapter.

Recently in **Normandy** <note>original: 'Normanden'</note>
A **monk** <note>original: 'Münch'</note> lived (I say without hesitation)
Who deceived in many ways
Many people, regardless of their rank;

**Sin, Knowledge, Joy, Pain, Normandy, Monk**

It serves as a warning that spiritual worthiness is required to access true knowledge, contrasted against the fraudulent actions of "false" alchemists.</summary>

However, after the year had ended,
All his art and power came to an end;
For when he pulled out his **glass** <note>Referring to the alchemical retort or 'philosophical egg' used to cook the metallic mixtures.</note>,
Of gold and copper, there was nothing at all <note>That is, the transmutation failed to produce the glowing stones or the desired gold; the materials were likely burnt or evaporated.</note>.
Therefore he became angry; very much
Did he stir himself with rage,
For all his goods and wealth
He had lost and squandered.

<summary>Roger Bacon becomes obsessed with finding or alchemically creating glowing carbuncles to light his bridge, but his year-long experiment fails. The page depicts his physical decline and his eventual rage upon realizing he has squandered his entire fortune on a failed transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, alchemy, carbuncles, transmutation, obsession, poverty, gold, copper</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse from the previous page. <page-num>63</page-num>

<meta>The page is enclosed in a decorative woodcut border consisting of oval and circular chain-like elements. Catchword "Vnd" (And) at bottom right.</meta>

But the true sons of the **Art** <note>original: 'Kunst'. Specifically the Alchemical or Hermetic Art.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who acknowledge God and stand before Him,
Without whom all things fail,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Always conduct themselves with due propriety:
Just as the **sciences** <note>original: 'Wissenschafftn'. Here meaning branches of knowledge or scholarly disciplines.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Have no beginning (consider this well)
Likewise, without Him,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They reach no good end:
You, the Most High, I confess before You,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That no one shall obtain such things hereafter,
Though he may have first studied them,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without You, nothing else here is of any value.
For with You, O God, there is always **grace**, <note>original: 'Gnad'. Divine favor, often cited by alchemists as a prerequisite for the 'Donum Dei' (Gift of God).</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To make the work perfect in deed,
As it is also received most swiftly,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For You are the beginning and the end. <note>original: 'das Haupt vnd das End'. Literally 'the head and the end,' a reference to the Alpha and Omega.</note>
Just as you have now heard
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of the joy of this most worthy Art,
So shall you now hear
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of the sorrow concerning the same;
Though it be the very opposite of the desire
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of one who delights himself in such an Art.

The first sorrow is surely this:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That one must realize in their **spirit** <note>original: 'Gmüth'. The heart, mind, or inner soul.</note> at once,
That many seek such an Art now,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But very few actually find it.

**Vocabulary:**
**Art** (Kunst), **God** (Gott), **Sciences** (Wissenschafftn), **Grace** (Gnad), **Sorrows** (Schmerzen), **Spirit/Mind** (Gmüth).

<summary>This page argues that true alchemical success is a divine gift, unattainable through mere study without God's grace. It concludes by transitioning to the 'sorrows' of the Art, specifically the rarity of actually achieving the work.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 63 regarding the "sorrows" or difficulties of the Alchemical Art. And so that no one may suddenly obtain
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Such **science**, <note>original: 'Wissenschafft'. In this period, the term refers to a systematic body of knowledge or a specific "sacred" craft, rather than modern laboratory science.</note>
Unless he be first learned in the matter
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Before he makes a beginning.
He is certainly a learned man
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who can speak with the right understanding.
He who can grasp it
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through the teaching of another—it helps him greatly,
For it is highly necessary
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That one might recognize so many diversities;

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of the secret works of **Nature**,

It may well happen that someone
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;According to it might also err immediately,

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who have now passed away,

Either in the first stage
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Or in the time that followed;
They all err (I say it without fear)
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Until the right **practice** <note>original: 'Practick'. The practical application or laboratory work, which alchemists often distinguished from 'Theoria' (theoretical study).</note> arrives.

When the **Artist**, <note>original: 'Künstler'. In this context, the practitioner of alchemy or the "Hermetic Art."</note> who at that time
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Is greedy, bold, and reckless.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ruins our **Work** <note>original: 'Werck'. The *Magnum Opus* or the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> sooner,

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To fulfill it in such a [reckless] way.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let him be cautious, above all things,

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He must begin again from the very start.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And be greatly troubled with sorrow.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To carry this out in **fantasies**. <note>original: 'Phantaseyn'. Delusions or mental imaginings that lead the practitioner away from the true laws of nature.</note>

<page-num>67</page-num>

Concerning the **virtue of the work** here, <note>original: 'Werck Tugend'. In alchemy, 'virtue' refers to the inherent power, efficacy, or moral quality of the substance and the process itself.</note>
Which you shall perform as is proper,
When you, through God's blessing and power,

The first danger now spoken of

Spoils and ruins them out of impatience.
All **authors**, who now
Truly write of this **Art**, <note>original: 'Authores' and 'Kunst'. Alchemy was known as 'The Art'.</note>

That humble book, as it is called,

Therefore, he **ripens** the work <note>original: 'maturirt' (from Latin 'maturare'). Alchemical processes were often compared to the slow ripening of fruit or the growth of an embryo.</note>

Well, according to the proverb, you shall
Experience the truth right here:
That the more one hurries,
The more sluggish he becomes in his spirit;
For whoever hurries in these things
Will not accomplish such a work;
Sometimes not in a month,
Nor does such happen in a year.

->Is truly difficult to believe,<-

->Yet may have more need of you, than you of him.<- <note>A warning against "Masters" who are actually seeking the student's money or resources to fund their own failed experiments.</note>

Before you shall take up and begin

->Moreover, worthy of great praise and honor;<-

Because of this **Medicine**,
   I was grieved in my spirit henceforth;
Therefore I thought well to myself

For in that very same **Abbey** there

Which runs and flows into a river,

As much as one would need at hand

Twenty thousand for the **army**

This I had long preserved

It was a foolish thing

And he desired in these matters

## The Third Chapter.

Tonsilus had labored
In the fire (as is proper)
For sixty years and even more,
So that he might attain his desire;
Another Englishman was
With Havvlton in the <term>West</term> <note>original: 'Occident'. This likely refers to Western England or the broader Western world.</note> there,
Always busy, as was required,
In exercising such <term>practice</term> <note>original: 'Practicam'. The hands-on, laboratory work of the alchemist.</note>,
Yet they never at any hour
Found such noble <term>science</term> <note>original: 'Wissenschafft'. The sacred and hidden knowledge of alchemy.</note>.

They knew not the <term>Matter</term> <note>original: 'Materiam'. The "First Matter" or "Prima Materia," the essential starting substance required for the alchemical Great Work.</note>,

In an improper manner they carried on /
And sought it constantly in many a place /
Until both their wealth and their life
Were squandered and had to be surrendered /
Much expense, anxiety, distress, and damage

Because he has now spent

Forever in false <term>recipes</term> <note>original: 'Recepten'. Formulas followed blindly without philosophical understanding.</note>,

In excrement—listen to me—
In antimony, arsenic,
In honey, wax, and wine also,

All things turned to nothing
In their effects, both great and small,

What he surely was undertaking.

### On the Proportion of Nature

Upon that, he thought artfully and well,
That nothing better would be fitting
Than to operate upon human blood:
Until I said that the blood
Entirely turns to smoke and spoils,
And by the most powerful fire
Is broken down monstrously <note>The Master explains that organic materials like blood are too volatile; they evaporate or "spoil" under the intense heat of the furnace rather than transforming into the Stone.</note>.
For the love of Christ, he said,
Instruct me and faithfully teach me
What truly and indeed should be
The fine **substance** of the Stone.
I said, <term>Tonsilus</term> <note>The name of the character, likely derived from the Latin 'tonsor' (barber), suggesting a lower-status practitioner or 'puffers' who lacked philosophical depth.</note>, listen to me:
What use is it to you, such an old man?
Cease from this matter hereafter,
And **dedicate yourself** <note>original: 'incumbir', from the Latin *incumbere*, meaning to apply oneself or devote oneself to a duty.</note> to prayers,
Because the time [of your life] requires it;
For if you knew, with certainty,
The **material** <note>original: 'Materiam'. Specifically the *Prima Materia*, the elusive starting substance of the alchemical work.</note> of our Stone,
Before you could have prepared it,
And before you had indeed **perfected** <note>original: 'perficirt'. The final stage of the alchemical process where the substance is brought to its highest, most 'perfect' state.</note> it,
You would lie there dead from old age.
He said: O my good master,
You need not be worried about that;
I would have sufficient comfort in this,
If I only knew the **material**.

<summary>The Master warns Tonsilus that experimenting with human blood is a futile "false path" and urges the elderly man to focus on his spiritual salvation rather than the Stone.</summary>
<keywords>human blood, alchemical failure, Tonsilus, Prima Materia, old age, spiritual vs. physical work, the Great Work</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Master and Tonsilus. The Master explains why the secrets of the Philosopher's Stone are so difficult to find in books, citing the intentional obscurity practiced by ancient authors to protect the "Art."</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>

Concerning the **Stone**, so often sought by me:
->I said: <term>Tonsilus</term> <note>As noted on the previous page, Tonsilus represents the aging, unsuccessful seeker of the Stone.</note>, I tell you:<-
It is certainly no small matter
->Which you desire to be certain of;<-
For many **authors** <note>original: 'Authores'. The Master refers to the "Alchemical Authorities" or "Adepts" of the past.</note> here and there
->Write much concerning this uncertainty,<-
But no one takes the pains
->To clearly prove such things from among them.<-
These same authors, at their pleasure,
->Who have written of this **Art**,<-
Report that they have prayed to God
->(As **Democritus** <note>Democritus (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) was a Greek philosopher. In alchemical tradition, he was often pseudo-epigraphically credited with early foundational texts like 'Physika kai Mystika'.</note> testifies)<-
That He might soon take from this world
->The souls of those who, in this manner,<-
Would write books about these matters,
->Or otherwise do the like;<-
But from those very ones now
->Many are equally careful likewise,<-
That they do not—more than is fitting—
->Hand down <note>original: 'tradirn', from the Latin *tradere*, to transmit or deliver.</note> this **science**,<-
Of which each learned man among them [noted]
->One or two points on the surface,<-
So that he might certainly be recognized
->By such companions <note>original: 'Gsellen'. Refers to fellow initiates or "brothers" in the alchemical craft.</note> without danger,<-
Because he is equal to them in quality,
->For one understands the other well.<-

<summary>The Master explains the tradition of alchemical secrecy, claiming that ancient authors like Democritus prayed for the deaths of those who would reveal the secret too clearly, and that they only leave subtle hints to recognize fellow initiates.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Tonsilus, Alchemical Secrecy, Democritus, Authors, Initiation, The Art, Hidden Knowledge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Master's explanation to Tonsilus. <page-num>92</page-num>

Thus they have written, not
That everyone might be instructed by them,
But that they reveal themselves rightly
Speaking to a secret lineage <note>original: 'gheymen Gschlecht'. This refers to the "sons of art" or the community of initiates who are spiritually prepared to understand the secrets.</note>
Therefore, do not be content
To read through only one book,
But read many fine **authors** <note>original: 'Authores'.</note> well,
Who are the best and most excellent;
One book opens another book;
The learned <term>Arnoldus</term> <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311) was a celebrated physician and alchemist whose works were foundational to the medieval alchemical tradition.</note> points to this,
As also <term>Anaxagoras</term> <note>An ancient Greek philosopher (c. 500–428 BC). Alchemists often cited him as an early authority who understood the underlying unity of matter.</note>
Testifies concerning this work.
For he who is lazy in his nature
To read through many books,
Will surely be considered
Quite unfit for the <term>Practica</term> <note>The 'Practice' refers to the actual laboratory work of alchemy, as opposed to mere theory.</note>.
But <term>Tonsilus</term>, listen dear friend,
I do not bring this forth as a mere alms <note>original: 'Allmoß'. The Master suggests this knowledge is a sacred trust, not a casual gift to be handed out like common charity.</note>
To reveal such a beautiful thing to someone
(Which has never happened before this time);
Through the use of plain answers
I will now give you good comfort,
If only you will ask me wisely.
"Good Master," he then said,
"Teach me certainly and truly:
Is <term>Sol</term> <note>original: 'Sol', the Latin word for Sun. In alchemical symbolism, Sol represents Gold.</note> the <term>Materia</term>? <note>original: 'Materia'. The 'Materia Prima' or 'First Matter' is the legendary starting material required to begin the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>"

<summary>The Master advises Tonsilus that alchemical secrets can only be decoded by comparing multiple authoritative texts, as "one book opens another." The dialogue shifts as Tonsilus begins to ask direct questions about the Materia Prima, specifically whether it is Gold (Sol).</summary>

"And then <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: 'Mercurius'. In alchemy, this refers both to the metal quicksilver and the philosophical principle of fluidity and transformation.</note> alongside?

Or only Mercury,

Or other minerals
To prepare our Stone?"
"Here," I said to him,
"O Tonsilus, are many questions,
Posed with wisdom
And not without subtlety:
You have not yet named it (understand),
Unless it was done in general terms <note>original: 'in genere'. Latin phrase meaning to speak of the general category or genus rather than the specific substance.</note>;

At different times, so that they
May be useful for the **Art** <note>The 'Art' refers to Alchemy, specifically the 'Great Work' of transmuting base matter into the Philosopher's Stone.</note> here;

<summary>Tonsilus questions the Master about the specific ingredients of the Philosopher’s Stone, listing Mercury, Gold, Silver, and Sulphur. Throughout all of Christendom,

<summary>The Master explains that the alchemical substance is not a commodity that can be bought, but must be personally prepared by a wise practitioner. Now I truly teach you,
In our language, what **Magnesia** <note>original: "Magnesi/Magnesia." A cryptic term for the "Mother" of the Stone, often described as a mystical lead or a transformative substance containing hidden potential.</note> is;
In Latin it is called here
Something great and wonderful.
The **copper** <note>original: "Kupffr." This refers to the alchemical *aes* (copper), which was often used as a pun for *aes* (money or value). The Master explains that while the material has physical value, its true worth lies in the knowledge it contains.</note> must truly be the money,
But the science is the **Art** <note>original: "Kunst." The "Great Art" (*Ars Magna*) of alchemy, which requires both practical skill and divine inspiration.</note>,
Which one receives through God's favor.
That is, that such a thing is this,
In which much divine knowledge is revealed.
Now you plainly recognize
Exactly what **Magnesia** is:
The matter of copper, in which with care
The Divine Wisdom lies hidden.
Therefore, my dear **Tonsilus** <note>original: "Tonsile." The name of the student or apprentice to whom the poem is addressed.</note>,
You must take both such stones
As the **materials** <note>original: "Materialia." The primary ingredients or the "Two Seeds" required to begin the alchemical process.</note>
To produce the **Elixir** <note>original: "Elixir." The final, perfected substance of the Great Work, capable of transmuting base metals or prolonging life.</note> there;
Even if, at first,
The materials are not many,
Nevertheless, many things help beforehand,
As I have told you before.
In truth, before this time, this **secret** <note>original: "Secret." A hidden truth or mystery intended only for the initiated.</note>
Was never made so manifest to anyone;
I have given the same to you.
I will pray to God that He does not [punish] me <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the author is likely expressing a common alchemical fear that revealing too many secrets to the unworthy could lead to divine punishment.</note>

# Chapter Four.

<image-desc>An alchemical woodcut illustration labeled with the number "13." in the upper left corner. It depicts a formal garden setting. To the right, a conjoined "hermetic" couple (Rebis) <term>The Rebis (from the Latin "res bina," meaning double matter) is an alchemical symbol representing the union of opposites—male and female, spirit and matter—resulting from the chemical wedding.</term> with two heads (male and female) lies on a mound beneath a tree. In the background are manicured hedges and conical trees.</image-desc>

Concerning the **Great Work** <note>original: "Das grosse Werck." Also known as the Magnum Opus, this refers to the multi-stage process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> from this point on,
I will not entirely hold back,
Although it may be a **secret** <note>original: "Secretum." Alchemists believed their knowledge was a sacred mystery that should only be shared with the worthy.</note>,
I will offer it abundantly and freely,
So that I may open the truth to you.
I will only attempt this
As far as I am permitted at this time
By **God's command** <note>original: "Gotts Befehl." This reflects the "Donum Dei" (Gift of God) concept: the belief that alchemical success was impossible without divine grace and moral purity.</note>.
I will now show you the way,
Just as a **guide** <note>original: "Führer."</note> is accustomed to do.

<summary>The author explains that this text serves as an "Ordinal" or essential manual of alchemy, filling in crucial details omitted by previous authorities. He warns that the practitioner's melancholy or mental distress can physically ruin the alchemical process.</summary>

## The Fifth Chapter

<image-desc>An alchemical woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame. When Briseus <note>Briseus: A figure often cited in alchemical literature (particularly Thomas Norton's *Ordinal*) who supposedly attempted to reform the coinage of his land. Here, he serves as an allegory for the alchemist's power to change the value and nature of metals.</note> undertook
->To change the coinage in the land,<-
Which to many was a ruin,
And to many was a great joy;
Although such a **mutation** <note>original Latin: *Mutatio*. A change or transformation, referring here to both the currency and the alchemical transmutation of metals.</note>
Was aimed at something else elsewhere,
Yet the matter was new and strange;
At that same time it was quite wonderful,
Which belongs, most wonderfully,
To this, our **science** <note>original: *Wissenschafft*. In the 15th-17th centuries, this term referred to a specialized body of knowledge or a "cunning art," specifically the art of alchemy.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from the previous discussion on "Multiplication." 1. Join your <term>Element: *Element*. While there are four physical elements, alchemists often spoke of the "One Element" or the unified substance resulting from their perfect combination.</term> together

And so that it corresponds;
Which rules and instructions indeed
Are there to help the learned;
They are noble instruments
In this work, at every turn;
Nothing can be more contrary to itself

In England and in France
Do not know how to teach you at all

Just as you shall see
In <term>Physics: *Physica*. In this period, "physics" referred broadly to the study of natural philosophy and the laws of the physical world.</term>, in natural matters;
In the *Book of the Tree* <note>original: *Buch vō baum*. Likely a reference to an alchemical allegory where the growth of the Stone is compared to the life cycle of a tree.</note> we have set it down.

2. Join them together also
According to the use of <term>Rhetoric: *Rhetoric* and *Redkunst*. Both terms refer to the art of persuasive speech. In alchemy, this is a metaphor for the harmonious "persuasion" or blending of different chemical natures.</term> and the art of speech,
With the natures that are there,
Ordered and finely purified;

Beautiful, bright, and also clear, manifest, and pure;
Come then, take the pure Earth,
The Water, Fire, and Air so worthy.

<summary>This page describes the "Rules of Conjunction," comparing the precise combination of alchemical elements to the scholarly rules of Grammar and Rhetoric. It emphasizes that the volatile and fixed parts of the work must be brought into "concordance" to create a pure Tincture.</summary>

3. The way of <term>Logic: *Logic*. In this context, the art of correct classification and reasoning applied to identifying the "seeds" or "genera" of the alchemical substances.</term> and the art of disputation

Has drawn many learned people in,
So that they ruined all their labor
And the Great Work all at once.

For two reasons here:

Which quite lovely, in deed,

6. Combined nimbly with <term>Astrology: *Astrologia*. The study of celestial movements, which alchemists believed governed the timing and success of their chemical reactions on Earth.</term>,

&emsp;Teach, are not the right ones there.

&emsp;And learn a new one in its place;
All the ancients shall at this hour
&emsp;Be found to have been deceived,
Because they placed two qualities
&emsp;Within a single <term>degree</term> <note>original: "Gradu"; a measurement of intensity in early science. The author argues against the idea that a substance can be, for example, equally cold and moist at the same level of intensity.</note> in this way;

&emsp;In our works, great or small;

&emsp;No one sings well or rightly;

Are very useful to recognize;

<summary>This page explains that the Philosopher's Stone is called a "Microcosm" because it harmonizes all the opposing qualities of the physical world—such as heat, cold, and volatility—into a single substance. ### Page 197
### n iij

And when our **Stone** <note>original: "Stein"; the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> from then on

Then its **Tincture** <note>original: "Tinctur"; the active medicinal or transmuting essence that "stains" or imparts its perfect quality to lesser substances.</note> shall extend

One part into a thousand parts henceforth;
Of which I myself am a witness.
Believe this only with total constancy
And also consider most firmly,
That it makes the silver as good
As **Minera** <note>original: "Minera"; raw ore or the natural mineral state as found in the earth. The author suggests the Stone produces silver of a quality equal to the finest natural deposits.</note> yields.
Thus our Stone shall from this point
Be **augmented** <note>original: "Augmentirt"; Augmentation is the alchemical process of increasing the quantity or the transformative power of the Stone after it has been achieved.</note> and grow greatly
In its size and its good nature,
Into that which has no end.
From this, its increase now,
As well as its **augmentation**,
Is compared not poorly to man
In his proper growth and creation.
Yet I wish to present one point
Of the truth here and now,
Which to some, according to the report,
May not be pleasing at all:
When, firstly, in such matters
You wish to make our **Stone** ready,
You should at that same time
Think of no personal profit.

<summary>This page describes the "multiplication" or augmentation of the Philosopher's Stone, explaining its ability to transform base metals at a ratio of 1 to 1000. It concludes with a moral warning that the alchemist must remain free from greed during the work.</summary>

<summary>The author explains that the final "Red" stage of the alchemical process is hidden within the "White" stage, citing authorities like the Turba Philosophorum, Maria the Jewess, and Hermes. He uses the metaphor of a marriage between a White Woman and a Red Man to describe the union of materials.</summary>

<keywords>Red Work, Rubedo, Philosophers' Stone, Turba Philosophorum, Maria the Jewess, Hermes Trismegistus, Chemical Wedding, Tincture</keywords>

# The Sixth Chapter

<image-desc>An alchemical engraving within a rectangular frame. On the left stands the figure of Hermes/Mercury, wearing a winged helmet and sandals, holding a caduceus. In the center is a brick furnace (athanor) with two round-bottomed flasks (cucurbits) on top, each fitted with a long vertical neck. On the right, a large lion stands on its hind legs, leaning its front paws against the furnace. In the background, a landscape with water and a distant town is visible.</image-desc>

<margin>26.</margin>

In regard to the matter <note>original: "Materiæ"</note>
And its agreement <term>The "concordance" (original: *Concordantzen*) refers to the harmonious union or compatibility between the different alchemical ingredients, ensuring they act as one.</term> (understand),
You shall observe therein
That there is no variation <note>In this context, "variation" or "alteration" (*Veränderung*) refers to an unwanted change or corruption that deviates from the unified state achieved in the previous stage.</note>
Between those very things, indeed,
Which are meant to come together there;
For surely from such variation
A discord <note>original: "Zwyspaltung"; literally a "splitting in two," referring to the failure of the substances to remain unified.</note> could arise.

<summary>This page concludes the previous discussion on the Red Work and begins Chapter Six, which focuses on the "concordance" or harmony required between alchemical substances. The text warns that any deviation or "variation" in the matter during this stage will result in a discord that ruins the union of the Stone.</summary>

<keywords>concordance, harmony, alchemical matter, discord, Hermes, athanor, Rubedo, unity</keywords>

Therefore your work, indeed,
->Would be done in vain,<-
Whoever wishes to begin our Work <term>The "Work" (German: *Werck*) refers to the *Magnum Opus* or Great Work of alchemy—the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</term>
->Let him mark these five rules,<-
Or five Concordances <term>From the Latin *concordantia*, meaning "agreements" or "harmonies." In alchemy, this refers to the necessary alignment between the practitioner, their tools, and the materials.</term>
->He shall observe in every measure:<-
The first rule, at this time,
->So that he may note, is this:<-
Whether his heart and mind <note>original: "Hertz vnd Gemüth"</note>
->Are in accord with the Work,<-
That it must rule and predominate <note>original: "prædominirn"</note>
->Above all else, as is proper;<-

->Will be made for naught and entirely in vain:<-
The second rule, of necessity,
->Is that one recognizes within himself<-

The third rule will already serve
->Your will and intention,<-
When the Work comes into agreement
->Well with the fine instruments:<-
The fourth Concordance
->Shall be researched well and truly,<-
In which place, or where,
->One can best arrange it so:<-

<summary>This page outlines the first four of five "rules" or "concordances" essential for alchemical success, emphasizing the spiritual alignment of the practitioner, the distinction between the art and the artist, and the suitability of the laboratory instruments and location.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the five essential rules or "concordances" for the alchemical Work. <page-num>209</page-num>

The fifth Rule <term>Original: *Regula*.</term> is entirely
->Concerning sympathy and concordance,<- <term>The German *Mittleiden* (literally "suffering with") here refers to "sympathy" in the classical sense: a natural resonance or connection between the earthly materials and the heavens.</term>
Between your cheerful Work
And the good heavenly Sphere. <term>The "heavenly sphere" (*Sphæra*) refers to the astrological movements and celestial influences believed to govern the success of the alchemical process.</term>
Of these five rules here
We now wish to treat;
Starting with the first one,
We will find it so happened
That truly few great people
Are constant in spirit <term>Original: *Gemüth*. This refers to the practitioner's psychological and spiritual state.</term> today,
Because they are completely overwhelmed,
And the Work is indeed long, for sure;
They want you to do
Only a forced violence to Nature; <note>A common alchemical warning: the practitioner must follow and assist Nature's natural pace rather than trying to force a result through excessive heat or "nothzwang" (compulsion).</note>
Some are newcomers,
Also the fire [is] so very quick, <note>This may refer to both the literal furnace fire and the figurative "fire" of the beginner's impatience.</note>
That after half a year
They wish or hope for nothing at all;
When barely a week has passed,
Some are such that their spirit and mind
Change, as I say now;
Some change it in a day;
Some believe firmly, and do not doubt,
That in one month it will be accomplished;
But in the following month, now,
They go and reject the entire Art. <term>The "Art" (*Kunst*) refers to alchemy itself, often called the "Hermetic Art."</term>

<summary>This page introduces the fifth alchemical rule regarding celestial harmony and begins a critique of impatient practitioners who lack the constancy of spirit required for the long duration of the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>Fifth Rule, sympathy, heavenly sphere, constancy of spirit, patience, nature, beginners, alchemy as Art</keywords>

# The Seventh Chapter.

<image-desc>An alchemical woodcut within a decorative border. On the left, a man in a tunic stands before a large brick furnace with a roaring fire. Nothing will now be able to hinder

As described by many authors,
Experienced and discovered before.

Like the cooks use (without harm)
->When they roast meat by the fire.<-

3. This same heat, truly,
Is required with the circular fire <term>Circular Fire: A method where the heat source (usually coals) is placed around the vessel rather than directly beneath it, ensuring an even, surrounding warmth.</term>
For the separation <note>original: "Einanderscheidung" – the process of dividing a substance into its different constituent parts or elements.</note>
Of the things to be divided.

4. But for the circulation <term>Circulation: A process in which a substance is heated so that it rises as vapor, condenses, and falls back down, repeatedly refining the material.</term>
Of the elements, a "white fire" <note>original: "weisses Fewer" – a specific intensity of heat. In alchemical traditions, "white" often refers to a heat stronger than the "slow" heat of digestion but not as intense as the "red" heat used for melting metals.</note>
Will be quite pleasant
And also very suitable for you;
Which fire, so prepared,
Should be equal and steady at all times,
In equal measure every minute,
Constant and without cease.
For such should never diminish,
Nor should it grow too much in that way;
Even though that same fire is good
And does not let up even once,
Yet study it wisely for that reason,
And observe everything from all sides,
So that you might properly
Obtain such a fire as well.
And in this same pure fire
There should be absolutely no moisture
That a hand might touch
Or an eye could even see.

5. Yet another "moist fire" <note>original: "feucht Fewr" – likely a reference to the "balneum Mariae" (water bath) or the heat generated by fermenting horse dung, providing a humid, gentle warmth.</note>
Is available and to be recognized at this time,

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on the management of alchemical fires. In a moment (even one so small)
The deficiency will not be enough: <note>The author warns that even a tiny error or "defect" in the heat can ruin the work; the margin for error is incredibly thin.</note>
Which will certainly be the case
To find the right degree there;
He will be a great, precious Master
In the future, truly in the fire;
It is difficult to <term>recognize</term> <note>original: "cognoscirn"; to perceive or identify through observation.</note> its power
Or to experience it quickly;
There is no method of testing it
Unless it be through the sight alone; <note>The alchemist must rely on their eyes—observing the color of the flame or the glow of the vessel—rather than any mechanical measurement.</note>
Therefore all err violently
In <term>expressing</term> <note>original: "exprimisn"; to describe or articulate the nature of the fire.</note> the same;
One learns of this warmth in time
Through diligence and experience;
Regarding this warmth, <term>Anaxagoras</term> <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher often cited in alchemical texts for his theories on the composition of matter and the "seeds" of things.</note>
Speaks specifically <note>original: "In Specie"</note> in every measure:
No one is found at the first hours
To be discerning and well-suited.
10. Another warmth at that time
Is of a great subjugation; <note>This refers to a more intense heat used to "subdue" or break the resistance of the materials.</note>
For the minerals, which alone
Are of a hard softening; <note>Likely referring to the annealing or melting of stubborn metallic ores.</note>
This warmth, as I report,
Cannot be too strong either;
Although it may be <term>continually</term> <note>original: "Continuè"</note>

<meta>This page continues the numbered list of the "warmths" or degrees of heat required in the alchemical process. While the previous page (234) discussed recognizing the intensity of the fire and its role in softening minerals, this page details the final specific stages of the work.</meta>

11. Another warmth is already found
Which is that of <term>Calcination</term>, <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or ash, removing impurities.</note>
For the impure metals as well,
And their preparation besides;
Which does not burn them,
Nor makes them too soft in the end;
Otherwise they would easily, with difficulty,
Be scattered and broken.

12. The twelfth warmth (hear me well) <note>original: "versteht mich schlecht"; an idiomatic expression meaning "mark my words" or "do not misunderstand me."</note>
Is rightly that of <term>Sublimation</term> <note>The process of vaporizing a substance so that it settles in a purified solid form in the upper part of a vessel.</note>
Of all <term>Spirits</term> <note>original: "Geister"; volatile substances like mercury or sulfur that "flee" the fire.</note> which, all together,
According to their nature, are mineral.

13. The final warmth at this time
Is certainly the most noble of all,
Since your Stone powerfully
Now approaches the <term>Projection</term> <note>original: "Wurff" (the Throw); the final stage where the Philosopher's Stone is "thrown" onto base metals to transform them into gold.</note> right well;
Practice makes a Master;
There is nothing more to be said,
Than he who is greedy, though he starts well,
Must necessarily start all over again. <note>The author warns that rushing or being driven by greed at the final stage will cause the work to fail, forcing the alchemist to begin the entire years-long process from scratch.</note>

Now I have taught you well
All things by their names,
Just as one person teaches another
The way to reach the city;
Through all villages and water too,
Over bridges and rough hills,

The author uses the metaphor of a traveler being guided to a city to represent the completion of the alchemist's education.</summary>

Just as the wise in these matters
->Happily complete their journey,<-

->Henceforth find through this <term>Art</term><note>The "Art" (German: Kunst) refers specifically to the Great Work of Alchemy.</note><-
