--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory framing of the alchemical work, emphasizing the authority and completeness of the "Ordinal." It addresses the reader directly, justifying the use of plain language (originally English in the source material this German text translates) to make the knowledge accessible to more than just the elite.</meta>

If you shall now fulfill
->All their teachings and deeds,<- <note>original: "Sentenz vnd Thun"; refers to the authoritative maxims and practical actions of the alchemical masters.</note>
Not through mere opinion alone,
->But through this <term>Ordinal</term>,<- <note>An "Ordinal" is a book prescribing a specific order or ritual. In this context, it refers to Thomas Norton's *Ordinal of Alchemy*, which provides a structured sequence for the Great Work.</note>
For within this Ordinal, truly,
->I take all doubt away from you;<-
Nothing is set down here that is not right,
->Nor has anything been omitted from it;<-
This day pleased me more
->When this teaching was given to me,<-
Than the sum of a thousand pounds
->(Which I now make known to another).<-
Three hundred pounds would not
->Have pleased me to take in exchange,<-
Had even this very small <term>chapter</term>
->Been omitted from this place.<- <note>original: "Capitul"; a specific section or division of the text.</note>
But so that you do not wonder,
->O good friends and noble hearts,<-
That such a noble <term>science</term>,
->As its power requires,<- <note>original: "Wissenschafft"; the systematic and spiritual knowledge required for alchemy.</note>
Is set down here quite rightly,
->Though in the English tongue quite plainly;<- <note>The author explains that the work was written in "Englischer Spraach" (English) rather than Latin. In the 15th century, writing such secrets in a common language was a deliberate choice to favor the "subtle" mind over mere status.</note>
For this has been done so
->That the multitude and the total sum<-
Of the common folk may be instructed;
->They are almost all of one worth.<- <note>The author suggests that by writing plainly, the knowledge is made available to the "coarse" or "common" people, asserting a spiritual equality in the pursuit of the Stone.</note>

<summary>The author asserts the absolute reliability and value of this specific alchemical guide (the Ordinal), claiming its knowledge is worth more than a fortune. He justifies writing the work in plain English (translated here from the German manuscript) to ensure the teachings are accessible to the common people.</summary>
<keywords>Ordinal, instruction, doubt, chapter, science, English language, common folk, value of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>This page concludes the German translation of the alchemical poem, following the author's justification for writing in a plain, accessible style. It serves as both a warning to the uninitiated and a formal colophon dating the work.</meta>

Ten thousand laymen in general <note>original: "Layen"; here referring to those without the specific "science" or secret knowledge of alchemy.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To every ten fine learned men/
From which great wealth every year
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In this land quite easily
Is ruined and completely passes away,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As the wise well understand,
And how many others besides,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of every rank and honor,
Are daily (consider this well)
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Brought to the deepest poverty: <note>A common theme in alchemical literature: the "puffers" or false alchemists who bankrupted themselves attempting to make gold without the true spiritual and chemical understanding.</note>
Desist, you laymen, desist in time,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So that you are not forever foolish;
It is much more useful to you
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To leave it alone now, than never at all:
All those who shall be pleased
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To look through this book with longing,
Shall pray for my soul,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For the dead and those still living.
After Christ fourteen hundred years,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Seventy and seven was the number <note>The year 1477. This marks the date Thomas Norton allegedly began his *Ordinal of Alchemy*.</note>
This work was begun, therefore
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**To GOD alone be praise, glory, and**
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**honor.**

->T H E &nbsp; E N D.<-

<summary>This concluding page of the manuscript warns unlearned "laymen" against the financial ruin of failed alchemy and dates the commencement of the work to 1477. It ends with a traditional pious dedication and a request for prayers for the author's soul.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Norton, Ordinal of Alchemy, 1477, laymen, poverty, spiritual alchemy, colophon, prayer for the dead</keywords>
